- **Result:** The net effect of desensitization and downregulation is a profound decrease in the ability of GnRH (or the agonist) to stimulate gonadotropin secretion. This leads to suppression of LH and FSH release, which in turn lowers sex steroid production (estradiol in females, testosterone in males), halting or slowing the progression of puberty. In CPP, this suppression slows bone age advancement and helps preserve final adult height.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1. The patient has CPP, characterized by premature activation of the HPG axis.
2. Treatment involves a continuous GnRH agonist (leuprolide).
3. The question asks for the mechanism of action of this treatment.
4. GnRH is normally secreted pulsatilely to stimulate gonadotrophs.
5. Continuous stimulation of GnRH receptors leads to desensitization (uncoupling from G protein) and downregulation (receptor internalization and degradation).
6. This reduces the responsiveness of gonadotrophs to GnRH/agonist, suppressing LH and FSH secretion.
7. Suppression of LH/FSH leads to decreased sex steroid production, halting pubertal progression.
8. Option (A) accurately describes this mechanism of receptor desensitization and downregulation.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Continuous (non-pulsatile) exposure uncouples and downregulates pituitary GnRH receptors, desensitizing gonadotrophs and profoundly suppressing LH and FSH secretion:** This is the correct mechanism. Continuous stimulation leads to receptor desensitization (uncoupling) and downregulation (reduced number), ultimately suppressing gonadotropin release.
- **(B) Leuprolide competitively blocks estrogen receptors in the breast and epiphyseal growth plates:** Leuprolide is a GnRH agonist, acting on pituitary GnRH receptors, not peripheral estrogen receptors. Anti-estrogens (like tamoxifen or selective estrogen receptor modulators - SERMs) block estrogen receptors. This option describes an incorrect mechanism and target.
- **(C) Long-acting GnRH agonists directly inhibit aromatase within the ovarian stroma:** Aromatase inhibitors (like anastrozole or letrozole) block the conversion of androgens to estrogens. GnRH agonists act on the pituitary, not directly on ovarian aromatase. This option describes an incorrect mechanism and target.
- **(D) Leuprolide stimulates the rapid synthesis of somatostatin to block all pituitary hormones:** Somatostatin is an inhibitor of GH and TSH secretion, but not primarily LH/FSH. While there might be some complex interactions, the primary mechanism of GnRH agonists is direct action on gonadotrophs via GnRH receptors, not indirect stimulation of somatostatin. This option describes an incorrect mechanism.
- **(E) Continuous GnRH agonists induce cytotoxic lysis of anterior pituitary lactotrophs:** Lactotrophs produce prolactin. GnRH agonists primarily target gonadotrophs (LH/FSH). While high doses or prolonged use might have complex effects, cytotoxic lysis of lactotrophs is not the intended or primary mechanism of action for treating CPP. This option describes an incorrect target cell type and mechanism.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)
| Feature             | Normal GnRH Secretion | Continuous GnRH Agonist Effect |
|----------------------|------------------------|---------------------------------|
| Secretion Pattern   | Pulsatile              | Continuous                      |
| Receptor State      | Sensitive             | Desensitized & Downregulated    |
| G-Protein Coupling  | Coupled                | Uncoupled                       |
| Intracellular Ca2+  | Increased              | Decreased                       |
| LH/FSH Secretion   | Stimulated             | Suppressed                      |
| Clinical Effect     | Puberty Progression    | Puberty Suppression             |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
Continuous administration of a GnRH agonist leads to desensitization and downregulation of pituitary GnRH receptors on gonadotrophs. This reduces the cells' responsiveness to GnRH, resulting in decreased LH and FSH secretion, which is the therapeutic basis for treating central precocious puberty and certain hormone-sensitive cancers (like prostate cancer). Understanding the difference between pulsatile (physiological) and continuous (pharmacological) GnRH stimulation is crucial.

---

### Question 110: Hypothalamic Hamartoma: Organic Central Precocious Puberty and Gelastic Epilepsy
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the etiology of central precocious puberty (CPP) and its association with specific hypothalamic lesions, particularly hypothalamic hamartomas, and their characteristic clinical presentation including gelastic seizures. It requires differentiating CPP causes based on clinical features and neuroimaging findings.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents with signs of precocious puberty, including accelerated growth, adult body odor, pubic hair (Tanner stage III), and penile enlargement (length: 8.5 cm, Tanner stage III). Physical examination reveals bilateral testicular enlargement to 6 mL (Tanner stage III). His mother reports a history since infancy of frequent, unprovoked episodes of inappropriate laughter lasting 15-30 seconds, often followed by brief confusion. Laboratory investigations show elevated serum LH and testosterone levels consistent with puberty. A GnRH stimulation test elicits a pubertal LH surge, confirming central precocious puberty. Which of the following neuroimaging findings is most likely present?

(A) A well-circumscribed, non-enhancing mass in the tuber cinereum / interpeduncular cistern on brain MRI (hypothalamic hamartoma)
(B) A cystic, calcified sellar mass compressing the optic chiasm (craniopharyngioma)
(C) Bilateral vestibular schwannomas in the cerebellopontine angle
(D) A pineal germinoma with obstructive hydrocephalus
(E) Cortical subependymal nodules with subungual fibromas

#### Correct Answer: A

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 3-year-old boy. Precocious puberty is defined as onset before age 9 in boys or 8 in girls. This patient clearly meets the criteria.
- **Signs of Precocious Puberty:** Accelerated growth, adult body odor, pubic hair (Tanner III), penile enlargement (8.5 cm, Tanner III), testicular enlargement (6 mL, Tanner III). These are classic signs of pubertal development.
- **Neurological Symptom:** Recurrent episodes of inappropriate laughter (gelastic seizures) followed by confusion since infancy. This is a highly specific symptom.
- **Endocrine Evaluation:** Elevated LH and testosterone (pubertal range). GnRH stimulation test shows a pubertal LH surge. This confirms *central* precocious puberty (CPP), meaning the problem originates in the hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal (HPG) axis, specifically premature activation of GnRH secretion.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the most likely neuroimaging finding given this clinical picture.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (生长激素、IGF-1与青春期发育分子机制推导)
- **Central Precocious Puberty (CPP):** Premature activation of the HPG axis. This can be idiopathic (most common) or secondary to identifiable CNS lesions.
- **Hypothalamic Hamartoma:** A non-neoplastic congenital malformation of the hypothalamus, typically located in the tuber cinereum or interpeduncular cistern. It is composed of disorganized neuronal tissue.
    - **Pathophysiology:** Hamartomas can contain GnRH-secreting neurons or neurons that secrete substances stimulating GnRH release. They are strongly associated with CPP and gelastic seizures. The mechanism for gelastic seizures is thought to involve aberrant stimulation of the limbic system, particularly the amygdala and hippocampus, by the hamartoma.
    - **Neuroimaging:** Typically appears as a well-circumscribed, non-enhancing mass on MRI, often isointense to gray matter on T1-weighted images and slightly hyperintense on T2-weighted images. It does not typically enhance with gadolinium contrast.
- **Craniopharyngioma:** A benign tumor arising from remnants of Rathke's pouch, usually located in the sellar/suprasellar region. Can cause CPP by compressing the hypothalamus or pituitary stalk, but typically presents with visual disturbances (optic chiasm compression), headaches, and endocrine deficiencies (hypopituitarism) in addition to or instead of precocious puberty. Often cystic and calcified on imaging.
- **Vestibular Schwannomas:** Tumors of the Schwann cells surrounding the vestibulocochlear nerve (CN VIII), typically located in the cerebellopontine angle. Associated with Neurofibromatosis type 2 (NF2). Not typically associated with CPP or gelastic seizures.
- **Pineal Germinoma:** A tumor arising from germ cells in the pineal gland. Can cause precocious puberty (rarely, via hypothalamic compression or GnRH secretion) but is more commonly associated with obstructive hydrocephalus, Parinaud syndrome (upward gaze palsy, pupillary light-near dissociation), and diabetes insipidus.
- **Tuberous Sclerosis Complex (TSC):** A genetic disorder characterized by hamartomas in multiple organs, including the brain (cortical tubers, subependymal nodules, subependymal giant cell astrocytomas - SEGAs), skin (facial angiofibromas, shagreen patch, hypomelanotic macules, ungual fibromas), kidneys (angiomyolipomas), heart (rhabdomyomas), and lungs (lymphangioleiomyomatosis). While CNS lesions are common, CPP is not a typical feature, and the characteristic findings are cortical tubers and subependymal nodules, not a hypothalamic hamartoma causing gelastic seizures and CPP.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1. The patient is a young boy (3 years old) presenting with clear signs of puberty (Tanner III genitalia and pubic hair, testicular enlargement). This indicates precocious puberty.
2. Endocrine testing (elevated LH/testosterone, pubertal GnRH response) confirms that the puberty is *central* (originating from the HPG axis).
3. The presence of gelastic seizures (inappropriate laughter) since infancy is a highly specific clinical feature.
4. The combination of central precocious puberty and gelastic seizures strongly points towards a specific underlying pathology: a hypothalamic hamartoma.
5. Evaluate the imaging options:
    - (A) Hypothalamic hamartoma: Matches the clinical picture (CPP + gelastic seizures). Location (tuber cinereum/interpeduncular cistern) and typical MRI appearance (non-enhancing mass) are consistent.
    - (B) Craniopharyngioma: Can cause CPP but is less commonly associated with gelastic seizures and often presents with other symptoms like visual deficits or hypopituitarism. Imaging features (cystic, calcified, sellar) differ.
    - (C) Vestibular schwannomas: Associated with NF2, not CPP or gelastic seizures. Location is different.
    - (D) Pineal germinoma: Can cause precocious puberty but is more associated with hydrocephalus and Parinaud syndrome. Location is different.
    - (E) Tuberous sclerosis findings: Not associated with CPP or gelastic seizures. Imaging findings are different.
6. Therefore, the most likely neuroimaging finding is a hypothalamic hamartoma.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) A well-circumscribed, non-enhancing mass in the tuber cinereum / interpeduncular cistern on brain MRI (hypothalamic hamartoma):** This is the correct answer. Hypothalamic hamartomas are the classic cause of the combination of central precocious puberty and gelastic seizures, especially when onset is in early childhood. The location and typical non-enhancing nature on MRI are characteristic.
- **(B) A cystic, calcified sellar mass compressing the optic chiasm (craniopharyngioma):** Craniopharyngiomas are common suprasellar masses in children but typically cause hypopituitarism and visual field defects. While they *can* cause CPP by hypothalamic compression, they are not associated with gelastic seizures. The imaging description (cystic, calcified, sellar) is also typical for craniopharyngioma, not hamartoma.
- **(C) Bilateral vestibular schwannomas in the cerebellopontine angle:** These are characteristic of Neurofibromatosis type 2. They cause hearing loss, tinnitus, and balance problems. They are not associated with CPP or gelastic seizures.
- **(D) A pineal germinoma with obstructive hydrocephalus:** Pineal germinomas are tumors of the pineal gland. They can cause precocious puberty (rarely) but are more commonly associated with signs of increased intracranial pressure (headache, vomiting) due to obstructive hydrocephalus and Parinaud syndrome (due to dorsal midbrain compression). Gelastic seizures are not a feature.
- **(E) Cortical subependymal nodules with subungual fibromas:** These are characteristic findings of Tuberous Sclerosis Complex (TSC). While TSC involves brain hamartomas, they are typically cortical tubers and subependymal nodules, not hypothalamic hamartomas causing CPP and gelastic seizures. CPP is not a typical feature of TSC.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)

| Condition                 | Key Clinical Features                                  | Neuroimaging Findings                                     | Association with CPP | Association with Gelastic Seizures |
| :------------------------ | :----------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------- | :-------------------------------- |
| Hypothalamic Hamartoma    | CPP, Gelastic Seizures (often since infancy)           | Non-enhancing mass, tuber cinereum/interpeduncular cistern | Yes                 | Yes                               |
| Craniopharyngioma         | Visual deficits, Hypopituitarism, Headaches, CPP (less common) | Cystic, calcified, sellar/suprasellar mass               | Possible            | No                                |
| Vestibular Schwannoma     | Hearing loss, Tinnitus, Balance issues                 | CPA mass                                                 | No                  | No                                |
| Pineal Germinoma          | Hydrocephalus, Parinaud Syndrome, Precocious Puberty (rare) | Pineal mass                                              | Rare                | No                                |
| Tuberous Sclerosis        | Skin lesions, Seizures (various types), Developmental delay | Cortical tubers, Subependymal nodules                    | No                  | No                                |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
The combination of central precocious puberty and gelastic seizures in a young child is highly suggestive of a hypothalamic hamartoma. This lesion is a congenital malformation located in the hypothalamus, typically appearing as a non-enhancing mass on MRI. Understanding the specific clinical syndromes associated with different CNS lesions is crucial for diagnosing the cause of central precocious puberty.

---

### Question 111: Peripheral Precocious Puberty: Autonomous Steroids, Suppressed LH/FSH, and Flat GnRH Test
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Differentiating central (GnRH-dependent) from peripheral (GnRH-independent) precocious puberty based on gonadotropin levels (LH, FSH) and response to GnRH stimulation, understanding the pathophysiology of autonomous steroid secretion and its effects on the hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal (HPG) axis.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 4-year-old boy presents with signs of virilization, including progressive penile enlargement, coarse pubic hair, and acne. Physical examination reveals Tanner stage III pubic hair and an 8-cm penis, consistent with pubertal development. However, testicular examination using a Prader orchidometer shows bilateral testicular volume of 2 mL, which is prepubertal (<4 mL). Bone age is advanced at 7.5 years, significantly exceeding his chronological age of 4 years. Endocrine laboratory results show markedly elevated serum testosterone (320 ng/dL, well above the pubertal range), but undetectable serum LH (<0.1 mIU/mL) and FSH (<0.1 mIU/mL). A GnRH stimulation test fails to elicit any significant rise in LH, with the peak LH level remaining flat at <0.2 mIU/mL.

Which of the following fundamental pathophysiological distinctions is demonstrated by this patient's clinical and laboratory profile?

(A) Peripheral precocious puberty (GnRH-independent); autonomous androgen secretion bypasses the hypothalamus, suppresses pituitary gonadotropins, and prevents testicular growth
(B) Central precocious puberty; hypothalamic GnRH hypersecretion causes selective testosterone release
(C) Constitutional delay of growth and puberty with paradoxical androgen surges
(D) Isolated growth hormone excess without gonadotropic involvement
(E) Primary hypogonadism with compensatory testicular atrophy

#### Correct Answer: A

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Age & Presentation:** 4-year-old boy with signs of puberty (penile enlargement, pubic hair, acne) - suggests precocious puberty.
- **Physical Exam:** Tanner III pubic hair, 8 cm penis (pubertal), but prepubertal testicular volume (2 mL) - discrepancy between virilization and testicular size is crucial. Testicular enlargement (>4 mL) is a hallmark of true puberty driven by gonadotropins.
- **Bone Age:** Advanced (7.5 years) - consistent with exposure to sex steroids, accelerating skeletal maturation.
- **Lab Results:**
    - High Testosterone (320 ng/dL): Confirms virilization.
    - Low LH & FSH (<0.1 mIU/mL): Indicates the pituitary is *not* producing gonadotropins in response to the high testosterone.
    - Flat GnRH Stimulation Test (Peak LH <0.2 mIU/mL): Confirms the pituitary is unresponsive to GnRH stimulation, further supporting suppressed gonadotropin secretion.
- **Interpretation:** The patient exhibits signs of puberty (advanced bone age, virilization) due to high testosterone, but the source is *not* the normal HPG axis activation. The pituitary gonadotropins (LH/FSH) are suppressed, and the pituitary does not respond to GnRH. This pattern points to autonomous (peripheral) production of sex steroids, independent of hypothalamic GnRH or pituitary gonadotropin release. The lack of testicular growth despite high testosterone confirms the absence of gonadotropic stimulation required for testicular maturation.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (生长激素、IGF-1与青春期发育分子机制推导)
- **Central Precocious Puberty (CPP):** Premature activation of the HPG axis. The hypothalamus releases GnRH prematurely, stimulating the pituitary to release LH and FSH. LH stimulates Leydig cells in the testes to produce testosterone, causing virilization and testicular growth. FSH stimulates Sertoli cells, contributing to spermatogenesis (though less relevant in young boys). Lab findings: Elevated LH/FSH (especially in response to GnRH stimulation), elevated testosterone, advanced bone age.
- **Peripheral Precocious Puberty (PPP):** Autonomous production of sex steroids from sources other than the normal HPG axis. This can be due to:
    - **Adrenal causes:** Congenital Adrenal Hyperplasia (CAH) due to 21-hydroxylase deficiency (most common), adrenal tumors secreting androgens.
    - **Gonadal causes:** Testicular tumors (Leydig cell tumors), familial male-limited precocious puberty (activating mutation in LH receptor on Leydig cells).
    - **Other causes:** Exogenous androgen exposure, McCune-Albright syndrome (activating GNAS mutation leading to autonomous endocrine function, including adrenal or gonadal).
- **Pathophysiology of PPP:** The autonomous source of androgens (e.g., adrenal tumor, Leydig cell tumor, activating LH receptor mutation) produces high levels of testosterone. This high testosterone exerts negative feedback on the hypothalamus (reducing GnRH) and the pituitary (reducing LH/FSH secretion). The pituitary becomes desensitized to GnRH stimulation. The testes remain small because they are not stimulated by LH/FSH.
- **GnRH Stimulation Test:** Used to differentiate CPP from PPP. In CPP, GnRH stimulation causes a significant rise in LH and FSH. In PPP, LH and FSH are already suppressed, and the pituitary is unresponsive, so the GnRH test shows a flat or minimal response.
- **This Case:** The patient has high testosterone, suppressed LH/FSH, and a flat GnRH response. This classic pattern definitively indicates PPP. The lack of testicular growth despite high testosterone confirms the absence of gonadotropin stimulation.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1.  **Identify the core problem:** Precocious puberty signs (penile growth, pubic hair, acne) in a 4-year-old boy.
2.  **Evaluate testicular development:** Prepubertal testicular size (2 mL) despite signs of puberty. This is a key finding suggesting the source of androgens is not the normal HPG axis activation.
3.  **Assess gonadotropin levels:** Undetectable LH and FSH. This indicates the pituitary is not driving the process.
4.  **Perform GnRH stimulation test:** Flat LH response. This confirms pituitary unresponsiveness, ruling out CPP where GnRH stimulation would cause LH/FSH rise.
5.  **Synthesize findings:** High testosterone + suppressed/unresponsive gonadotropins + lack of testicular growth = Peripheral Precocious Puberty (GnRH-independent). The high testosterone is coming from an autonomous source (adrenal or gonadal tumor, activating mutation, etc.) and is suppressing the HPG axis.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Peripheral precocious puberty (GnRH-independent); autonomous androgen secretion bypasses the hypothalamus, suppresses pituitary gonadotropins, and prevents testicular growth:** This option accurately describes the patient's condition. High testosterone from an autonomous source suppresses LH/FSH and prevents testicular growth. This matches the clinical and lab findings perfectly.
- **(B) Central precocious puberty; hypothalamic GnRH hypersecretion causes selective testosterone release:** Incorrect. CPP involves premature HPG axis activation, leading to *elevated* LH/FSH (especially after GnRH stimulation) and testicular growth. This patient has suppressed LH/FSH and no testicular growth. The GnRH stimulation test would be positive in CPP.
- **(C) Constitutional delay of growth and puberty with paradoxical androgen surges:** Incorrect. Constitutional delay is characterized by *delayed* puberty and growth, not precocious puberty. While some individuals might have transient androgen surges, this doesn't explain the sustained virilization, advanced bone age, suppressed gonadotropins, and flat GnRH response seen here.
- **(D) Isolated growth hormone excess without gonadotropic involvement:** Incorrect. GH excess causes acromegaly/gigantism, not precocious puberty with virilization. While GH can indirectly influence puberty, isolated GH excess does not cause high testosterone, suppressed gonadotropins, or a flat GnRH response.
- **(E) Primary hypogonadism with compensatory testicular atrophy:** Incorrect. Primary hypogonadism (e.g., Klinefelter syndrome) involves testicular failure, leading to *low* testosterone and *high* LH/FSH (hypergonadotropic hypogonadism) due to lack of negative feedback. This patient has high testosterone and suppressed LH/FSH.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)

| Feature             | Central Precocious Puberty (CPP) | Peripheral Precocious Puberty (PPP) |
| :------------------ | :------------------------------- | :-------------------------------- |
| Cause               | Premature HPG axis activation    | Autonomous steroid source         |
| Testosterone        | Elevated                         | Elevated                          |
| LH/FSH              | Elevated (especially post-GnRH)   | Suppressed                        |
| GnRH Test Response  | Positive (LH/FSH rise)           | Flat/Minimal                      |
| Testicular Size     | Pubertal                         | Prepubertal                       |
| Bone Age            | Advanced                         | Advanced                          |
| Example Causes      | Idiopathic, CNS lesions          | CAH, Adrenal/Testicular tumors, McCune-Albright |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
The key distinction between central and peripheral precocious puberty lies in the status of the hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal (HPG) axis. Central precocious puberty involves premature activation of the HPG axis, leading to elevated gonadotropins (LH/FSH) and testicular growth. Peripheral precocious puberty involves autonomous production of sex steroids outside the HPG axis, which suppresses gonadotropin secretion and prevents testicular growth. A flat LH response to GnRH stimulation is diagnostic for peripheral precocious puberty.

---

### Question 112: McCune-Albright Syndrome: GNAS Postzygotic Mosaicism and the Classical Triad
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: McCune-Albright syndrome (MAS) is a rare genetic disorder characterized by the classic triad of polyostotic fibrous dysplasia, café-au-lait macules, and endocrine hyperfunction, caused by a postzygotic activating mutation in the GNAS gene.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 5-year-old girl presents to the emergency department with acute vaginal bleeding. Her physical examination reveals unilateral breast development (right Tanner stage III, left Tanner stage I), multiple large, irregularly bordered hyperpigmented macules ("coast of Maine") on the right side of her back and buttocks, and a history of a pathological fracture of the right femur. Pelvic ultrasound shows a solitary 3.5-cm right ovarian cyst and an enlarged endometrial stripe. Laboratory tests reveal elevated serum estradiol (85 pg/mL) with suppressed LH and FSH. Skeletal radiographs show polyostotic fibrous dysplasia characterized by expansile, radiolucent "ground-glass" lesions with cortical thinning. What is the genetic basis of this patient's condition?

(A) Postzygotic somatic mosaic activating mutation in the GNAS gene encoding the Gs-alpha subunit, causing constitutive ligand-independent adenylyl cyclase activation
(B) Germline loss-of-function mutation in the MEN1 tumor suppressor gene
(C) Autosomal dominant mutation in the RET proto-oncogene
(D) Trinucleotide CGG repeat expansion in the FMR1 gene
(E) Deletion of the maternal copy of chromosome 15q11-q13

#### Correct Answer: A

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Age/Sex:** 5-year-old girl. Precocious puberty is a key feature.
- **Presenting Complaint:** Vaginal bleeding. Suggests precocious puberty due to estrogen exposure.
- **Physical Exam:**
    - Unilateral breast development (Tanner III right, I left): Suggests peripheral precocious puberty, likely estrogen-driven, potentially from an autonomous ovarian source.
    - Café-au-lait macules ("coast of Maine"): Characteristic skin finding in MAS, typically unilateral and with irregular borders.
    - Pathological fracture (right femur): Suggests underlying bone abnormality, consistent with fibrous dysplasia.
- **Imaging:**
    - Pelvic Ultrasound: Ovarian cyst (3.5 cm) and enlarged endometrial stripe. Consistent with estrogen production leading to endometrial proliferation.
    - Skeletal Radiographs: Polyostotic fibrous dysplasia ("ground-glass" lesions). Characteristic bone finding in MAS.
- **Labs:**
    - Estradiol: Elevated (85 pg/mL). Confirms estrogen excess.
    - LH/FSH: Suppressed. Indicates peripheral precocious puberty (gonadal autonomy), not central (pituitary-driven).
- **Diagnosis:** The combination of polyostotic fibrous dysplasia, café-au-lait macules, and peripheral precocious puberty strongly points to McCune-Albright syndrome (MAS). The question asks for the genetic cause.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (生长激素、IGF-1与青春期发育分子机制推导)
- **McCune-Albright Syndrome (MAS):** A rare disorder resulting from a postzygotic somatic activating mutation in the *GNAS* gene.
- **GNAS Gene:** Encodes the alpha subunit of the stimulatory G protein (Gsα). This protein is crucial for signal transduction pathways involving G protein-coupled receptors (GPCRs).
- **Gsα Function:** When a GPCR is activated by its ligand (e.g., hormones like LH, FSH, TSH, PTH, GHRH), it undergoes a conformational change, activating Gsα. Activated Gsα then activates adenylyl cyclase, which converts ATP to cyclic AMP (cAMP). cAMP acts as a second messenger, activating protein kinase A (PKA) and initiating downstream cellular responses.
- **MAS Mutation:** The mutation in *GNAS* in MAS (typically R201C or R201H) results in a constitutively active Gsα protein. This means the protein is always "on," continuously activating adenylyl cyclase even in the absence of ligand binding to the GPCR.
- **Consequences of Constitutive Gsα Activation:**
    - **Endocrine Tissues:** In endocrine glands (like the ovary in this case), constitutive activation leads to autonomous hormone production (estrogen in this case) independent of normal pituitary regulation (hence suppressed LH/FSH). This causes peripheral precocious puberty. Other endocrine manifestations include hyperthyroidism, Cushing syndrome (adrenal adenoma), growth hormone excess (pituitary adenoma), and hyperprolactinemia.
    - **Bone:** In bone, constitutive Gsα activation in osteoblast precursors leads to abnormal bone development, resulting in fibrous dysplasia.
    - **Skin:** The mechanism for café-au-lait macules is less clear but involves melanocyte dysfunction due to the Gsα mutation.
- **Postzygotic Mosaicism:** The mutation occurs *after* fertilization, typically during early embryonic development. This means that only some cells in the body carry the mutation, leading to a mosaic pattern of affected tissues (e.g., unilateral skin lesions, unilateral fibrous dysplasia, autonomous function in one ovary). This explains why the symptoms are often unilateral or patchy.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1.  **Identify the Syndrome:** The patient presents with the classic triad of MAS: polyostotic fibrous dysplasia (pathological fracture, radiographic findings), café-au-lait macules (unilateral, irregular borders), and endocrine hyperfunction (precocious puberty with vaginal bleeding, unilateral breast development, elevated estradiol, suppressed LH/FSH).
2.  **Determine the Type of Precocious Puberty:** The elevated estradiol with suppressed gonadotropins (LH/FSH) indicates peripheral precocious puberty, meaning the ovaries are producing estrogen autonomously, independent of pituitary stimulation.
3.  **Recall the Genetic Basis of MAS:** MAS is known to be caused by a specific genetic defect.
4.  **Evaluate the Options:**
    - (A) Describes the known genetic cause of MAS: a postzygotic activating mutation in *GNAS* leading to constitutive Gsα activity. This fits the clinical picture perfectly.
    - (B) MEN1 mutations cause Multiple Endocrine Neoplasia type 1, involving pituitary, parathyroid, and pancreatic tumors, not the triad seen here.
    - (C) RET mutations cause Multiple Endocrine Neoplasia type 2 (MEN2) and familial medullary thyroid carcinoma, not MAS.
    - (D) FMR1 CGG repeat expansion causes Fragile X syndrome, a neurodevelopmental disorder.
    - (E) Deletion of maternal 15q11-q13 causes Prader-Willi syndrome, characterized by hypotonia, hyperphagia, obesity, and developmental delay.
5.  **Confirm the Correct Answer:** Option (A) accurately describes the genetic etiology of McCune-Albright syndrome, explaining the autonomous endocrine function (peripheral precocious puberty), fibrous dysplasia, and café-au-lait spots.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Postzygotic somatic mosaic activating mutation in the GNAS gene encoding the Gs-alpha subunit, causing constitutive ligand-independent adenylyl cyclase activation:** This is the correct answer. It accurately describes the molecular basis of MAS, explaining the autonomous function of affected tissues (ovary, bone, melanocytes) due to persistent cAMP signaling.
- **(B) Germline loss-of-function mutation in the MEN1 tumor suppressor gene:** This causes Multiple Endocrine Neoplasia type 1 (MEN1), characterized by tumors of the parathyroid glands, anterior pituitary, and pancreatic islet cells. It does not cause fibrous dysplasia or the specific café-au-lait macules of MAS. The inheritance pattern is germline, not postzygotic mosaic.
- **(C) Autosomal dominant mutation in the RET proto-oncogene:** This causes Multiple Endocrine Neoplasia type 2 (MEN2), characterized by medullary thyroid carcinoma, pheochromocytoma, and parathyroid hyperplasia/adenoma. It is unrelated to fibrous dysplasia or the specific features of MAS.
- **(D) Trinucleotide CGG repeat expansion in the FMR1 gene:** This causes Fragile X syndrome, the most common inherited cause of intellectual disability, associated with characteristic physical features and behavioral problems. It is unrelated to MAS.
- **(E) Deletion of the maternal copy of chromosome 15q11-q13:** This causes Prader-Willi syndrome, characterized by hypotonia, feeding difficulties in infancy followed by hyperphagia and obesity, short stature, hypogonadism, and developmental delay. It is unrelated to MAS.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)

| Feature             | McCune-Albright Syndrome (MAS)                                  | MEN1                                       | MEN2                                       | Fragile X Syndrome                       | Prader-Willi Syndrome                     |
| :------------------ | :------------------------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------- |
| **Genetic Cause**   | Postzygotic *GNAS* activating mutation (Gsα)                   | Germline *MEN1* loss-of-function            | Germline *RET* activating mutation          | FMR1 CGG repeat expansion                | 15q11-q13 maternal deletion/paternal UPD |
| **Key Features**    | Fibrous dysplasia, Café-au-lait (Coast of Maine), Precocious Puberty | Parathyroid adenomas, Pituitary adenomas, Pancreatic NETs | Medullary thyroid carcinoma, Pheochromocytoma | Intellectual disability, Long face, Macroorchidism | Hypotonia, Hyperphagia, Obesity, Short Stature |
| **Inheritance**     | Somatic mosaic                                                 | Autosomal Dominant                          | Autosomal Dominant                          | X-linked Dominant                        | Imprinting disorder                      |
| **Gsα Involvement** | Direct cause (constitutive activation)                          | No                                         | No                                         | No                                       | No                                       |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
McCune-Albright syndrome is a classic example of a disorder caused by a postzygotic somatic activating mutation. The mutation in the *GNAS* gene leads to constitutive activation of the Gsα protein, resulting in autonomous endocrine function (like peripheral precocious puberty), fibrous dysplasia, and characteristic skin pigmentation, all occurring in a mosaic pattern. Understanding this specific genetic defect and its downstream effects is crucial for diagnosing and managing MAS.

---

### Question 113: Testotoxicosis: Activating Mutation in the Luteinizing Hormone Receptor (LHCGR)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The question tests the understanding of the molecular basis of testotoxicosis caused by an activating mutation in the LH receptor (LHCGR), specifically why this condition manifests precociously in males but not in heterozygous females, linking genotype to phenotype in the context of gonadal steroidogenesis and puberty.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents with rapid penile growth, pubic hair, aggressive behavior, and accelerated height velocity. His father and paternal uncle experienced identical rapid sexual maturation at age 3. Physical examination reveals Tanner stage III pubic hair and a 9-cm phallus, but symmetric prepubertal to minimally enlarged testes (3 mL each). Laboratory testing demonstrates a serum testosterone of 380 ng/dL (normal prepubertal range: <50 ng/dL), with undetectable baseline and GnRH-stimulated LH and FSH. Genetic sequencing reveals a heterozygous activating missense mutation (Asp578Gly) in the LHCGR gene encoding the LH receptor. Why does this activating mutation cause precocious puberty exclusively in males and not in heterozygous female family members?

(A) Constitutive LHCGR activation in testicular Leydig cells drives autonomous testosterone synthesis without LH; in females, ovarian estrogen synthesis requires dual FSH and LH receptor activation (two-cell, two-gonadotropin model)
(B) The LHCGR gene is located strictly on the non-pseudoautosomal region of the Y chromosome
(C) Females possess a circulating antibody that neutralizes the mutated LH receptor
(D) Ovarian theca cells lack G-protein coupled receptors during childhood
(E) In females, estrogen receptor beta suppresses the mutated LH receptor in trans

#### Correct Answer: A

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old boy with signs of precocious puberty (penile growth, pubic hair, aggression, accelerated growth). Family history suggests autosomal dominant inheritance.
- **Physical Exam:** Tanner III pubic hair, significantly enlarged phallus (9 cm), but small testes (3 mL). This discrepancy (large penis, small testes) is characteristic of gonadotropin-independent precocious puberty, specifically testotoxicosis.
- **Laboratory Findings:** Markedly elevated testosterone (380 ng/dL), suppressed LH and FSH (undetectable baseline and after GnRH stimulation). This confirms gonadotropin-independent (peripheral) precocious puberty, where the gonads are stimulated directly, leading to high sex steroid levels and subsequent negative feedback suppression of the HPG axis.
- **Genetic Finding:** Heterozygous activating mutation (Asp578Gly) in the LHCGR gene. This mutation causes constitutive activation of the LH receptor, mimicking the effect of LH binding.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the reason why this autosomal dominant mutation causes precocious puberty in males but not in heterozygous females.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (生长激素、IGF-1与青春期发育分子机制推导)
- **LHCGR Activation & Testotoxicosis:** The activating mutation in LHCGR leads to constitutive activation of the receptor, primarily in Leydig cells of the testes. This mimics LH binding, triggering the Gs-adenylyl cyclase-cAMP-PKA pathway, leading to increased steroidogenesis, specifically testosterone production. This autonomous testosterone production causes the signs of precocious puberty in males.
- **Gonadal Steroidogenesis:**
    - **Males (Testes):** Leydig cells, stimulated by LH (or constitutively activated LHCGR in this case), produce testosterone. Sertoli cells, stimulated by FSH, support spermatogenesis and produce inhibin B and androgen-binding protein (ABP). Testosterone is the primary driver of male secondary sexual characteristics.
    - **Females (Ovaries):** Theca cells, stimulated by LH, produce androgens (primarily androstenedione and testosterone). Granulosa cells, stimulated by FSH, convert these androgens into estrogens (primarily estradiol) via aromatase. This two-cell, two-gonadotropin model is crucial for estrogen synthesis. Estrogen is the primary driver of female secondary sexual characteristics.
- **Why Males are Affected, Females are Not:** In males, Leydig cells express LH receptors. Constitutive activation of these receptors by the mutated LHCGR leads to autonomous testosterone production, causing precocious puberty. In females, theca cells also express LH receptors, but granulosa cells express FSH receptors. Estrogen synthesis requires coordinated stimulation of both theca cells (by LH) and granulosa cells (by FSH). While the mutated LHCGR in theca cells might lead to increased androgen production, this is insufficient to cause significant precocious puberty in females. The lack of FSH stimulation (which is also suppressed due to negative feedback from the androgens) prevents efficient conversion of androgens to estrogens by granulosa cells. Furthermore, the threshold for estrogen production required to trigger precocious puberty is significantly higher than the threshold for testosterone production in males. The small amount of androgens produced by theca cells is not sufficient to overcome the negative feedback on the HPG axis or to trigger significant pubertal development.
- **Receptor Signaling:** LHCGR is a G-protein coupled receptor (GPCR). Activating mutations often lead to constitutive Gs protein activation, resulting in increased cAMP and downstream signaling even in the absence of LH.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1. The patient presents with signs of precocious puberty (penile enlargement, pubic hair) and elevated testosterone.
2. Suppressed LH and FSH indicate gonadotropin-independent (peripheral) precocious puberty.
3. The activating mutation in LHCGR explains the autonomous stimulation of Leydig cells in males, leading to high testosterone.
4. The question asks why heterozygous females are unaffected.
5. Consider the role of LH and FSH in gonadal steroidogenesis. In males, LH stimulates Leydig cells to produce testosterone. In females, LH stimulates theca cells to produce androgens, while FSH stimulates granulosa cells to convert androgens to estrogens.
6. Evaluate the effect of the mutated LHCGR in females. It would activate theca cells, potentially increasing androgen production.
7. However, estrogen synthesis requires both LH (theca cells) and FSH (granulosa cells). Since the HPG axis is suppressed in both sexes due to high sex steroids, FSH levels are low.
8. Therefore, even with activated LH receptors in theca cells, the lack of FSH stimulation prevents significant estrogen production in granulosa cells.
9. The amount of androgen produced by theca cells is insufficient to cause precocious puberty in females.
10. This explains why the condition manifests in males (testosterone-driven puberty) but not in heterozygous females (estrogen synthesis requires both LH and FSH stimulation, and FSH is suppressed).

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Constitutive LHCGR activation in testicular Leydig cells drives autonomous testosterone synthesis without LH; in females, ovarian estrogen synthesis requires dual FSH and LH receptor activation (two-cell, two-gonadotropin model).** This is the correct explanation. The mutation causes autonomous testosterone production in males. In females, estrogen synthesis requires both LH (theca cells) and FSH (granulosa cells), and the suppression of FSH due to negative feedback prevents significant estrogen production despite potential LH receptor activation in theca cells.
- **(B) The LHCGR gene is located strictly on the non-pseudoautosomal region of the Y chromosome.** This is incorrect. The LHCGR gene is located on chromosome 2p21, an autosome. Therefore, it can be inherited in an autosomal dominant pattern, explaining the family history.
- **(C) Females possess a circulating antibody that neutralizes the mutated LH receptor.** This is incorrect. There is no evidence for such an antibody. The lack of effect in females is due to the physiological requirements for estrogen synthesis, not an immunological phenomenon.
- **(D) Ovarian theca cells lack G-protein coupled receptors during childhood.** This is incorrect. Theca cells express LH receptors (a GPCR) throughout life, including childhood. These receptors are necessary for androgen production.
- **(E) In females, estrogen receptor beta suppresses the mutated LH receptor in trans.** This is incorrect. While estrogen receptor beta (ERβ) exists and can have complex interactions, it does not suppress the mutated LHCGR in a way that explains the lack of phenotype in heterozygous females. The primary reason is the requirement for FSH stimulation for estrogen synthesis.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)

| Feature             | Males (Testotoxicosis)                                  | Females (Heterozygous)                               |
|----------------------|--------------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------|
| Mutation Effect     | Constitutive LHCGR activation in Leydig cells            | Constitutive LHCGR activation in theca cells          |
| Primary Hormone     | Testosterone                                            | Androgens (primarily)                                 |
| Gonadotropin Status | Suppressed LH/FSH                                       | Suppressed LH/FSH                                     |
| Key Requirement     | LH stimulation of Leydig cells for Testosterone          | LH stimulation of theca cells AND FSH stimulation of granulosa cells for Estrogen |
| Outcome             | Precocious Puberty (Testosterone-driven)                | No significant precocious puberty (Insufficient Estrogen) |
| Receptor Location   | Leydig Cells (LHCGR)                                    | Theca Cells (LHCGR), Granulosa Cells (FSHR)           |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
Activating mutations in the LHCGR gene cause testotoxicosis in males due to autonomous Leydig cell stimulation and testosterone production. In heterozygous females, while theca cell androgen production may increase, estrogen synthesis requires coordinated stimulation by both LH (acting on theca cells via LHCGR) and FSH (acting on granulosa cells via FSHR). Suppression of FSH due to negative feedback from the increased androgens prevents significant estrogen production, thus explaining the lack of precocious puberty phenotype in females. This highlights the distinct requirements for steroidogenesis in males and females and the importance of gonadotropin interplay.

---

### Question 114: 21-Hydroxylase Deficiency: Shunting to Adrenal Androgens and Precocious Pubarche
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Congenital Adrenal Hyperplasia (CAH) due to 21-hydroxylase deficiency, leading to impaired cortisol and aldosterone synthesis, shunting precursors towards adrenal androgen production, resulting in precocious pubarche and virilization.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 5-year-old boy presents with progressive pubic hair development, penile enlargement, and a rapid growth spurt. Physical examination reveals Tanner stage III pubic hair, a normal stretched penile length of 8.5 cm, and bilaterally small, prepubertal testes (2 mL each). He has no history of salt-wasting crises or hypotension in infancy. Radiographs show a bone age advanced by 3.5 years. Laboratory testing demonstrates:
- Serum 17-Hydroxyprogesterone (17-OHP): Markedly elevated (4,200 ng/dL; normal: <100)
- Serum Androstenedione and DHEA-S: Markedly elevated
- Serum Testosterone: Elevated (180 ng/dL)
- Serum LH and FSH: Undetectable

Which of the following enzyme deficiencies accounts for this clinical presentation (simple virilizing CAH)?

(A) 21-Hydroxylase (CYP21A2)
(B) 17-alpha-Hydroxylase (CYP17A1)
(C) Aromatase (CYP19A1)
(D) 3-beta-Hydroxysteroid dehydrogenase (HSD3B2)
(E) Cholesterol side-chain cleavage enzyme (CYP11A1)

#### Correct Answer: A

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 5-year-old boy with signs of precocious puberty: pubic hair (Tanner III), penile enlargement (stretched penile length 8.5 cm, normal for age), rapid growth spurt (bone age advanced by 3.5 years).
- **Physical Exam:** Small, prepubertal testes (2 mL) are crucial. This indicates the source of androgens is peripheral (adrenal) rather than gonadal (testicular). Normal testicular size would suggest central precocious puberty or gonadotropin-independent testicular puberty.
- **History:** No history of salt-wasting crises suggests a non-salt-wasting form of CAH, although salt-wasting can occur in severe forms of 21-hydroxylase deficiency.
- **Laboratory Findings:**
    - Markedly elevated 17-OHP (4,200 ng/dL) is the hallmark diagnostic marker for 21-hydroxylase deficiency.
    - Elevated androstenedione and DHEA-S confirm adrenal androgen excess.
    - Elevated testosterone is consistent with peripheral conversion of adrenal androgens or direct adrenal production.
    - Undetectable LH and FSH indicate the precocious puberty is gonadotropin-independent (peripheral).
- **Synthesis:** The combination of peripheral virilization (pubic hair, penile enlargement, advanced bone age), small testes, elevated adrenal androgens (DHEA-S, androstenedione, testosterone), and markedly elevated 17-OHP points strongly towards congenital adrenal hyperplasia (CAH) due to 21-hydroxylase deficiency. This is the "simple virilizing" form, as there's no mention of salt wasting, although it's possible the patient has a milder form.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (生长激素、IGF-1与青春期发育分子机制推导)
- **Steroidogenesis Pathway:** Cholesterol -> Pregnenolone (CYP11A1) -> Progesterone -> 17-OHP (CYP21A2) -> 11-Deoxycortisol -> Cortisol (CYP21A2) AND Progesterone -> 17-OHP (CYP21A2) -> Androstenedione -> Testosterone.
- **21-Hydroxylase (CYP21A2) Deficiency:** This enzyme catalyzes two crucial steps: conversion of progesterone to 17-OHP and 11-deoxycortisol to cortisol. Deficiency leads to:
    - Decreased cortisol production -> Lack of negative feedback on the HPA axis -> Increased ACTH secretion.
    - Decreased aldosterone production (in severe forms) -> Salt wasting, hyponatremia, hyperkalemia.
    - Accumulation of precursors proximal to the block (progesterone, 17-OHP).
    - Shunting of these precursors down the androgen pathway -> Increased production of adrenal androgens (DHEA, DHEA-S, androstenedione, testosterone).
- **Other Enzyme Deficiencies:**
    - **17-alpha-Hydroxylase (CYP17A1) Deficiency:** Blocks production of cortisol, sex steroids (androgens and estrogens), and aldosterone precursors. Leads to hypertension (due to mineralocorticoid excess from DOC accumulation) and lack of virilization (or feminization in females). 17-OHP levels would be low.
    - **Aromatase (CYP19A1) Deficiency:** Blocks conversion of androgens to estrogens. Leads to virilization in females and lack of secondary sexual characteristics in males. 17-OHP levels would be normal.
    - **3-beta-Hydroxysteroid dehydrogenase (HSD3B2) Deficiency:** Blocks conversion of pregnenolone to progesterone, 17-OHP to 11-deoxycortisol, and DHEA to androstenedione. Leads to accumulation of DHEA (a weak androgen) and tetrahydrodeoxycorticosterone (DOC, a mineralocorticoid). Causes ambiguous genitalia in females, mild virilization in males, and hypertension. 17-OHP levels would be low.
    - **Cholesterol side-chain cleavage enzyme (CYP11A1) Deficiency:** Blocks the first step of steroidogenesis. Leads to severe deficiency of all steroid hormones (glucocorticoids, mineralocorticoids, androgens). Presents with adrenal insufficiency and ambiguous genitalia. 17-OHP levels would be low.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's presentation (precocious pubarche, advanced bone age, elevated adrenal androgens, small testes, undetectable gonadotropins) and the markedly elevated 17-OHP level are pathognomonic for 21-hydroxylase deficiency.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1.  Identify the core problem: Precocious puberty signs (pubic hair, penile enlargement, advanced bone age) in a young boy.
2.  Determine the source of puberty: Small testes and undetectable LH/FSH rule out central precocious puberty and testicular puberty. This points to a peripheral source of androgens.
3.  Consider peripheral androgen sources: Adrenal glands are the most likely source in this context.
4.  Evaluate laboratory findings: Elevated adrenal androgens (DHEA-S, androstenedione, testosterone) confirm adrenal hyperfunction. Markedly elevated 17-OHP is a specific marker for a block in the cortisol synthesis pathway.
5.  Correlate findings with enzyme deficiencies: The specific pattern of elevated 17-OHP and adrenal androgens, along with the clinical picture, directly implicates 21-hydroxylase deficiency.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) 21-Hydroxylase (CYP21A2):** Correct. Deficiency leads to decreased cortisol and aldosterone (potentially), shunting precursors to adrenal androgens. The markedly elevated 17-OHP is the key diagnostic finding. This fits the clinical picture perfectly.
- **(B) 17-alpha-Hydroxylase (CYP17A1):** Incorrect. Deficiency impairs cortisol and sex steroid synthesis. Patients typically present with hypertension (due to DOC accumulation) and lack of virilization (or feminization in females). 17-OHP levels would be low.
- **(C) Aromatase (CYP19A1):** Incorrect. Deficiency impairs estrogen synthesis. Leads to virilization in females and lack of secondary sexual characteristics in males. Does not cause elevated 17-OHP or the specific pattern of adrenal androgen excess seen here.
- **(D) 3-beta-Hydroxysteroid dehydrogenase (HSD3B2):** Incorrect. Deficiency impairs synthesis of cortisol, aldosterone, and potent androgens. Leads to accumulation of DHEA (weak androgen) and DOC (mineralocorticoid). Causes ambiguous genitalia in females, mild virilization in males, and hypertension. 17-OHP levels would be low.
- **(E) Cholesterol side-chain cleavage enzyme (CYP11A1):** Incorrect. Deficiency blocks the first step of steroidogenesis, leading to severe deficiency of all steroid hormones. Presents with adrenal insufficiency and ambiguous genitalia. 17-OHP levels would be low.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)
| Enzyme Deficiency        | Key Hormones Affected | Key Lab Finding | Clinical Features                                                                 |
| :----------------------- | :-------------------- | :-------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| 21-Hydroxylase (CYP21A2) | Cortisol, Aldosterone | ↑ 17-OHP        | Precocious pubarche, virilization, salt-wasting (sometimes), hypertension (sometimes) |
| 17-alpha-Hydroxylase (CYP17A1) | Cortisol, Sex Steroids, Aldosterone precursors | ↓ 17-OHP        | Hypertension, lack of virilization (or feminization in females)                    |
| Aromatase (CYP19A1)      | Estrogens             | Normal 17-OHP   | Virilization in females, lack of secondary sex characteristics in males           |
| 3-beta-HSD (HSD3B2)      | Cortisol, Aldosterone, Potent Androgens | ↓ 17-OHP        | Ambiguous genitalia (females), mild virilization (males), hypertension              |
| CYP11A1                  | All Steroids          | ↓ 17-OHP        | Adrenal insufficiency, ambiguous genitalia                                         |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
Congenital adrenal hyperplasia (CAH) is a group of autosomal recessive disorders caused by deficiencies in enzymes required for cortisol synthesis. The most common form (90-95%) is 21-hydroxylase deficiency, characterized by decreased cortisol and aldosterone production and increased adrenal androgen production, leading to virilization. Markedly elevated 17-OHP levels are diagnostic. Understanding the steroidogenesis pathway and the specific consequences of each enzyme deficiency is crucial for diagnosing CAH and differentiating its various forms.

---

### Question 115: Granulosa Cell Tumor: Isosexual Precocious Pseudopuberty, Call-Exner Bodies, and Inhibin
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of pediatric endocrine disorders, specifically isosexual precocious puberty caused by an ovarian tumor, the characteristic histological features of granulosa cell tumors (Call-Exner bodies), and the associated hormonal profile (high estradiol, high inhibin B, suppressed gonadotropins). It also requires differentiating between various ovarian tumors based on clinical presentation, hormonal findings, and histology.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-year-old girl presents with progressive breast development (thelarche, Tanner stage III), vaginal discharge, and subsequent monthly vaginal bleeding (menarche). Her height velocity is accelerated. Pelvic examination reveals a 6-cm solid and cystic left adnexal mass. Transabdominal ultrasound confirms the mass and shows an abnormally thickened endometrial stripe, consistent with estrogenic stimulation. Laboratory tests show markedly elevated serum estradiol (180 pg/mL; normal prepubertal <10 pg/mL, pubertal 20-200 pg/mL) and elevated serum inhibin B (450 pg/mL; normal prepubertal <100 pg/mL, pubertal 100-400 pg/mL). Serum LH and FSH levels are suppressed (<0.1 mIU/mL; normal prepubertal 0.1-1.0 mIU/mL). Surgical resection of the ovarian mass reveals tumor cells arranged in microfollicular clusters surrounding eosinophilic fluid-filled spaces, a histological pattern known as Call-Exner bodies. What is the definitive diagnosis?

(A) Juvenile granulosa cell tumor of the ovary
(B) Dysgerminoma
(C) Sertoli-Leydig cell tumor
(D) Mature cystic teratoma (dermoid cyst)
(E) Yolk sac tumor (endodermal sinus tumor)

#### Correct Answer: A

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 6-year-old girl presenting with signs of precocious puberty (breast development, vaginal bleeding). This immediately points towards an endocrine abnormality causing early activation of the hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal (HPG) axis or peripheral estrogen production.
- **Physical Exam:** Adnexal mass (6 cm) suggests an ovarian source. Thickened endometrium confirms estrogenic effect.
- **Hormonal Profile:**
    - **High Estradiol (180 pg/mL):** Confirms estrogen excess as the cause of precocious puberty.
    - **High Inhibin B (450 pg/mL):** Inhibin B is specifically produced by granulosa cells. Elevated levels strongly suggest a granulosa cell tumor.
    - **Suppressed LH/FSH (<0.1 mIU/mL):** Indicates that the precocious puberty is *peripheral* (gonadotropin-independent) rather than *central* (gonadotropin-dependent). The high estrogen levels from the tumor exert negative feedback on the pituitary, suppressing gonadotropin release. This pattern is characteristic of isosexual precocious *pseudopuberty* caused by estrogen-secreting ovarian tumors.
- **Histology:** Call-Exner bodies are pathognomonic for granulosa cell tumors. These are microfollicular structures containing eosinophilic material, representing degenerated granulosa cells within the tumor.
- **Differential Diagnosis:** The combination of isosexual precocious puberty, high estradiol, high inhibin B, suppressed gonadotropins, and Call-Exner bodies points definitively to a granulosa cell tumor.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (生长激素、IGF-1与青春期发育分子机制推导)
- **Granulosa Cell Tumors:** These are sex cord-stromal tumors derived from granulosa cells of the ovarian follicle. They are the most common malignant ovarian tumor in postmenopausal women but can occur at any age, including childhood (juvenile granulosa cell tumor).
- **Hormone Production:** Granulosa cells are responsible for producing estradiol (via aromatase converting androgens) and inhibin (specifically inhibin B). Therefore, granulosa cell tumors typically secrete large amounts of estrogen and inhibin B.
- **Mechanism of Precocious Puberty:** The excessive estrogen production by the tumor leads to premature development of secondary sexual characteristics (breast development, vaginal bleeding) and accelerated growth. Because the estrogen source is peripheral (the tumor), it suppresses the HPG axis via negative feedback, leading to low LH and FSH levels. This is termed isosexual precocious *pseudopuberty*.
- **Inhibin B:** Inhibin B is a glycoprotein hormone produced by granulosa cells in males and females. It selectively inhibits FSH secretion from the pituitary gonadotrophs. Elevated inhibin B is a key diagnostic marker for granulosa cell tumors.
- **Call-Exner Bodies:** These are characteristic microscopic features of granulosa cell tumors, representing granulosa cells arranged around a central eosinophilic substance. They are highly specific for this tumor type.
- **Differential Diagnosis Mechanisms:**
    - **Dysgerminoma:** A germ cell tumor, typically presents as an abdominal mass, often with elevated LDH and hCG (if syncytiotrophoblastic giant cells are present). Does not produce significant estrogen or inhibin B.
    - **Sertoli-Leydig Cell Tumor:** A sex cord-stromal tumor containing both Sertoli and Leydig cells. Produces androgens (testosterone) and estrogen (via peripheral aromatization). Causes virilization (hirsutism, clitoromegaly, voice deepening) and potentially isosexual precocious puberty, but typically has lower inhibin B and higher testosterone levels than a granulosa cell tumor.
    - **Mature Cystic Teratoma (Dermoid Cyst):** A germ cell tumor containing tissues from all three germ layers (ectoderm, mesoderm, endoderm). Usually benign. Does not typically cause precocious puberty unless it contains functional thyroid tissue (leading to hyperthyroidism) or rarely, functional ovarian tissue.
    - **Yolk Sac Tumor (Endodermal Sinus Tumor):** A malignant germ cell tumor. Produces alpha-fetoprotein (AFP) and often hCG. Does not produce estrogen or inhibin B.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1.  **Identify the core problem:** Precocious puberty in a 6-year-old girl.
2.  **Determine the type of precocious puberty:** Signs (breast development, vaginal bleeding) indicate estrogen excess. Suppressed LH/FSH indicate peripheral (gonadotropin-independent) cause. This is isosexual precocious *pseudopuberty*.
3.  **Localize the source:** Adnexal mass points to an ovarian origin.
4.  **Analyze hormonal profile:** High estradiol confirms estrogen excess. High inhibin B specifically points towards granulosa cell origin. Suppressed LH/FSH confirms peripheral source.
5.  **Analyze histology:** Call-Exner bodies are pathognomonic for granulosa cell tumor.
6.  **Synthesize findings:** The clinical presentation (isosexual precocious pseudopuberty), hormonal profile (high estradiol, high inhibin B, low LH/FSH), and histology (Call-Exner bodies) are all consistent with a granulosa cell tumor.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Juvenile granulosa cell tumor of the ovary:** This is the correct diagnosis. It fits all the clinical features: isosexual precocious puberty due to estrogen secretion, suppressed gonadotropins, elevated inhibin B (specific marker), and the characteristic Call-Exner bodies on histology.
- **(B) Dysgerminoma:** This is a germ cell tumor. It typically does not produce significant estrogen or inhibin B. Precocious puberty is not a typical presentation. Histology shows large, uniform cells with clear cytoplasm and prominent nucleoli, often with lymphocytes.
- **(C) Sertoli-Leydig cell tumor:** This is a sex cord-stromal tumor that produces androgens and some estrogen. It typically causes virilization (masculinization) and potentially isosexual precocious puberty. However, testosterone levels would likely be elevated, and inhibin B levels are usually not as high as in granulosa cell tumors. Histology shows tubules resembling seminiferous tubules (Sertoli cells) and clusters of Leydig cells.
- **(D) Mature cystic teratoma (dermoid cyst):** This is the most common ovarian germ cell tumor. It is usually benign and does not typically cause precocious puberty unless it contains ectopic functional tissue (e.g., thyroid). Hormonal profile would not show high estradiol and inhibin B. Histology shows various mature tissues (hair, teeth, bone, skin).
- **(E) Yolk sac tumor (endodermal sinus tumor):** This is a malignant germ cell tumor. It produces AFP and sometimes hCG. It does not produce estrogen or inhibin B. Precocious puberty is not a typical presentation. Histology shows Schiller-Duval bodies (glomerulus-like structures).

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)

| Feature                 | Granulosa Cell Tumor | Dysgerminoma | Sertoli-Leydig Cell Tumor | Mature Teratoma | Yolk Sac Tumor |
| :---------------------- | :------------------- | :----------- | :------------------------ | :-------------- | :------------- |
| **Age**                 | Any (incl. juvenile) | Young adult  | Any                      | Any             | Child/Young Adult |
| **Presentation**        | Precocious Puberty   | Abdominal Mass | Virilization/Puberty      | Often Asymptomatic | Abdominal Mass |
| **Estrogen**            | High                 | Low          | Variable (low-mod)        | Low             | Low            |
| **Inhibin B**           | High                 | Low          | Low-Mod                  | Low             | Low            |
| **Testosterone**        | Low-Mod              | Low          | High                     | Low             | Low            |
| **LH/FSH**              | Suppressed           | Normal/High  | Normal/High               | Normal          | Normal         |
| **Tumor Markers**       | Inhibin B            | LDH, hCG     | None specific             | None            | AFP, hCG       |
| **Histology**           | Call-Exner Bodies    | Clear Cells  | Sertoli/Leydig tubules    | Mature Tissues  | Schiller-Duval Bodies |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
This case highlights the presentation of isosexual precocious pseudopuberty caused by an estrogen-secreting ovarian tumor. Key diagnostic features include elevated estradiol, suppressed gonadotropins (LH/FSH), elevated inhibin B (specific for granulosa cell origin), and characteristic histology (Call-Exner bodies). This distinguishes it from central precocious puberty (elevated LH/FSH) and other ovarian tumors like Sertoli-Leydig (virilization, high testosterone), dysgerminoma (no estrogen/inhibin B), teratoma (usually asymptomatic), or yolk sac tumor (AFP elevation).

---

### Question 116: Leydig Cell Tumor: Unilateral Asymmetric Testicular Enlargement and Reinke Crystals
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of causes of precocious puberty in boys, specifically peripheral (gonadotropin-independent) precocious puberty due to a testicular tumor, and the characteristic histological features of Leydig cell tumors. It also assesses the interpretation of hormonal profiles (high testosterone, suppressed LH/FSH) in this context.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 7-year-old boy presents with signs of virilization including pubic hair, facial acne, and voice deepening, along with accelerated growth. Physical examination reveals Tanner stage III pubic hair and a large phallus, consistent with precocious puberty. Scrotal examination shows significant asymmetry: the left testis is small and prepubertal (2 mL), while the right testis is enlarged (8 mL total volume) and contains a firm, non-tender, discrete 2.5-cm intratesticular nodule. Laboratory investigations show markedly elevated serum testosterone (520 ng/dL; normal prepubertal range <20 ng/dL, pubertal range 300-1000 ng/dL), with suppressed serum LH and FSH levels (indicating gonadotropin independence). A testicular biopsy of the nodule reveals polygonal eosinophilic cells with round central nuclei and characteristic rod-shaped cytoplasmic crystalloids of Reinke. What is the diagnosis?

(A) Leydig cell tumor of the testis
(B) Seminoma
(C) Embryonal carcinoma
(D) Adrenal rest tumor secondary to uncontrolled CAH
(E) Choriocarcinoma

#### Correct Answer: A

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Age and Presentation:** 7-year-old boy presenting with signs of precocious puberty (pubic hair, acne, voice deepening, accelerated growth). This immediately points towards an endocrine cause.
- **Physical Exam:** Tanner stage III pubic hair and large phallus confirm virilization. Crucially, the scrotal exam reveals *asymmetric* testicular enlargement, with one testis (left) being prepubertal (2 mL) and the other (right) enlarged (8 mL) containing a palpable nodule. This asymmetry strongly suggests a unilateral testicular pathology causing the precocity.
- **Laboratory Findings:** Markedly elevated testosterone (520 ng/dL) confirms hyperandrogenism. Suppressed LH and FSH levels indicate that the source of testosterone is *independent* of pituitary gonadotropin stimulation (gonadotropin-independent precocious puberty).
- **Histopathology:** Testicular biopsy shows polygonal eosinophilic cells with round central nuclei and Reinke crystals. This is the pathognomonic histological description of Leydig cells, and the presence of a tumor composed of these cells is diagnostic.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (生长激素、IGF-1与青春期发育分子机制推导)
- **Precocious Puberty:** Development of secondary sexual characteristics before age 8 in boys or age 10 in girls. Can be central (GnRH-dependent, elevated LH/FSH) or peripheral (GnRH-independent, suppressed LH/FSH).
- **Peripheral Precocious Puberty in Boys:** Caused by autonomous production of sex steroids (testosterone) from the testes or adrenal glands, or exogenous exposure. Common causes include Leydig cell tumors, Sertoli cell tumors (rarely), adrenal tumors (rarely), congenital adrenal hyperplasia (CAH), McCune-Albright syndrome, or exogenous androgens.
- **Leydig Cell Tumors:** Neoplasms arising from testicular Leydig cells, which normally produce testosterone. These tumors can autonomously secrete large amounts of testosterone, leading to peripheral precocious puberty in boys. They typically present as a palpable testicular mass, often unilateral.
- **Hormonal Profile:** In Leydig cell tumors causing precocious puberty, the high intratumoral testosterone production suppresses the hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal (HPG) axis via negative feedback, leading to low LH and FSH levels.
- **Histopathology of Leydig Cell Tumor:** Characterized by polygonal cells with abundant eosinophilic cytoplasm, round central nuclei, and distinctive rod-shaped cytoplasmic crystalloids of Reinke (calcium-bound lipoproteins).
- **Differential Diagnosis:**
    - **Seminoma/Embryonal Carcinoma:** Germ cell tumors. Seminomas are typically homogenous sheets of large cells with clear cytoplasm and prominent nucleoli. Embryonal carcinomas are more aggressive, with primitive cells forming glands, rosettes, and papillary structures. Neither typically causes precocious puberty directly (though some germ cell tumors can secrete hCG, which stimulates Leydig cells, but this would usually lead to bilateral enlargement and elevated LH).
    - **Adrenal Rest Tumor (ART) secondary to CAH:** ARTs are nodules of adrenal tissue found within the testes, often associated with CAH. In uncontrolled CAH (e.g., 21-hydroxylase deficiency), adrenal androgens (like DHEA-S) are elevated, which can be peripherally converted to testosterone, potentially causing precocious puberty. However, ARTs themselves don't typically produce testosterone directly in large amounts, and the primary hormonal abnormality in CAH is elevated adrenal androgens, not just testosterone, and often involves elevated ACTH. The histology described (Reinke crystals) is specific to Leydig cells, not adrenal rest tissue.
    - **Choriocarcinoma:** A malignant germ cell tumor that produces high levels of human chorionic gonadotropin (hCG). hCG stimulates Leydig cells in the testes, leading to increased testosterone production and potentially precocious puberty. However, choriocarcinoma typically presents with systemic symptoms (metastases, hemorrhage) and elevated hCG levels. The histology is characterized by syncytiotrophoblasts and cytotrophoblasts, not Leydig cells with Reinke crystals. Also, hCG stimulation would typically lead to *elevated* LH levels (due to structural similarity), although FSH might be suppressed. The vignette explicitly states suppressed LH/FSH.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1.  **Identify the core problem:** Precocious puberty in a boy (Tanner III pubic hair, large phallus, voice change, acne).
2.  **Analyze physical exam:** Unilateral testicular enlargement with a palpable nodule, and contralateral small testis. This points to a unilateral testicular source.
3.  **Interpret lab results:** Very high testosterone confirms hyperandrogenism. Suppressed LH/FSH indicates gonadotropin-independent (peripheral) precocious puberty.
4.  **Consider differential diagnoses for peripheral precocious puberty:** Testicular tumors (Leydig, Sertoli), adrenal tumors, CAH, exogenous androgens.
5.  **Evaluate the histology:** Polygonal eosinophilic cells with round nuclei and Reinke crystals are pathognomonic for Leydig cells.
6.  **Synthesize findings:** A unilateral testicular mass composed of Leydig cells, causing autonomous testosterone production and suppressed gonadotropins, perfectly fits the description of a Leydig cell tumor.
7.  **Rule out other options:** Seminoma/Embryonal carcinoma have different histology and typically don't cause this hormonal profile. Adrenal rest tumor is associated with CAH and doesn't have Reinke crystals. Choriocarcinoma produces hCG, which would likely elevate LH, not suppress it, and has different histology.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Leydig cell tumor of the testis:** This is the correct diagnosis. The clinical presentation (unilateral testicular mass, precocious puberty), hormonal profile (high testosterone, suppressed LH/FSH), and pathognomonic histology (Leydig cells with Reinke crystals) all align perfectly.
- **(B) Seminoma:** A common type of germ cell tumor. Histologically characterized by large cells with clear cytoplasm and prominent nucleoli. Does not typically cause precocious puberty directly, and the histology described is incorrect.
- **(C) Embryonal carcinoma:** A malignant germ cell tumor. Histologically shows primitive cells forming glands, rosettes, etc. Does not typically cause precocious puberty directly, and the histology described is incorrect.
- **(D) Adrenal rest tumor secondary to uncontrolled CAH:** ARTs are nodules of adrenal tissue in the testes, often seen in CAH. While CAH can cause precocious puberty due to excess adrenal androgens, the primary source is adrenal, not testicular. ARTs themselves don't usually produce significant testosterone. The histology described (Reinke crystals) is specific to Leydig cells, not adrenal rest tissue.
- **(E) Choriocarcinoma:** A malignant germ cell tumor that secretes hCG. hCG stimulates Leydig cells, potentially causing precocious puberty. However, hCG stimulation typically leads to *elevated* LH levels (due to structural similarity), not suppressed levels as seen here. Furthermore, the histology is characterized by cytotrophoblasts and syncytiotrophoblasts, not Leydig cells with Reinke crystals.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)

| Feature                 | Leydig Cell Tumor          | Seminoma                 | Embryonal Carcinoma       | Adrenal Rest Tumor (CAH) | Choriocarcinoma        |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------- | :----------------------- | :----------------------- | :----------------------- | :--------------------- |
| **Presentation**        | Precocious puberty, mass   | Painless mass           | Painful mass, mets       | Precocious puberty (CAH) | Mets, gynecomastia    |
| **Testosterone**        | High (autonomous)          | Normal                  | Normal                  | Variable (adrenal)       | High (via LH/hCG)      |
| **LH/FSH**              | Suppressed                | Normal/High (if mets)    | Normal/High (if mets)    | Variable (CAH)           | Elevated (hCG effect)  |
| **Histology**           | Leydig cells, Reinke crystals | Large cells, clear cytoplasm | Primitive cells, glands | Adrenal tissue          | Syncytiotrophoblasts   |
| **hCG**                 | Normal                     | Normal                  | Normal                  | Normal                  | Very High              |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
Peripheral precocious puberty in boys is often caused by autonomous testosterone production. A unilateral testicular mass with suppressed LH/FSH strongly suggests a testicular tumor, most commonly a Leydig cell tumor. The characteristic histology of Leydig cells, including Reinke crystals, confirms this diagnosis. Understanding the hormonal profiles (high testosterone, suppressed gonadotropins) and histological features is crucial for differentiating testicular causes of precocious puberty.

---

### Question 117: hCG Cross-Activation of Leydig Cell LH Receptors: Hepatoblastoma and Pineal Germinoma
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the molecular mechanism by which human chorionic gonadotropin (hCG), secreted by certain tumors (like hepatoblastoma or germinomas), causes isosexual precocious puberty in boys (via Leydig cell stimulation) but not in girls (lack of similar cross-activation of ovarian function). It requires knowledge of hormone receptor cross-reactivity, gonadotropin actions, and sex-specific endocrine physiology.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 5-year-old boy presents with signs of precocious puberty, including penile enlargement (8 cm phallus, Tanner stage III), pubic hair (Tanner stage III), and rapid linear growth. Physical examination reveals mildly enlarged testes (5 mL bilaterally). An abdominal mass is palpated in the right upper quadrant. Laboratory tests show elevated serum testosterone (280 ng/dL), undetectable serum LH (<0.1 mIU/mL), markedly elevated serum beta-hCG (12,000 mIU/mL), and markedly elevated serum alpha-fetoprotein (AFP). Liver biopsy confirms a hepatoblastoma. The question asks for the molecular mechanism explaining why hCG-secreting tumors cause isosexual precocious puberty in boys but not in girls.

(A) hCG binds directly to testicular LH receptors on Leydig cells, stimulating testosterone secretion; in females, hCG cannot induce precocious puberty because ovarian estrogen production requires both LH and FSH
(B) The female liver lacks enzymes required to synthesize beta-hCG
(C) Ovarian follicles undergo immediate apoptosis when exposed to hCG
(D) hCG is rapidly degraded by placental aromatase in female children
(E) Leydig cells express human chorionic gonadotropin receptors that are absent in female ovaries

#### Correct Answer: (E)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 5-year-old boy with signs of precocious puberty (penile enlargement, pubic hair, rapid growth). This suggests excess sex steroid production.
- **Physical Exam:** Tanner stage III genitalia and pubic hair, mildly enlarged testes (5 mL). This confirms pubertal development.
- **Laboratory Findings:**
    - Elevated Testosterone (280 ng/dL): Confirms androgen excess, driving the virilization.
